## 15     [EMAIL_REDACTED]

16 [SOURCE: Page_166]

17     Abstract Water-level fluctuations are a major component of the functioning of artificial systems such as [SOURCE: Page_166]

18     reservoirs. Such fluctuations had great impacts on the littoral zones of reservoirs, zones that have a high diversity [SOURCE: Page_166]

19     of habitat and are of great importance for fish species in achieving their biological cycles. Working on the [SOURCE: Page_166]

20     Bariousses, a French reservoir, we studied the influence of water-level fluctuations on intra-annual variation of [SOURCE: Page_166]

21     littoral habitat availability (at different water levels) and their consequences on the structure of fish assemblages [SOURCE: Page_166]

22     inhabiting these areas. As the water level decreased, even slightly, the habitat diversity decreased and the littoral [SOURCE: Page_166]

23     zone became increasingly homogeneous. The temporal variability of habitat conditions had significant [SOURCE: Page_166]

24     consequences on the balance of the factors controlling the structure of fish assemblages. At intermediate levels, [SOURCE: Page_166]

25     habitat was a determining factor, whereas at high and low water levels other mechanisms such as interspecific [SOURCE: Page_166]

26     interactions may have been the main drivers. [SOURCE: Page_166]

27 [SOURCE: Page_166]

28     Keywords Reservoir – Littoral zone - Habitat - Fish - WLF [SOURCE: Page_166]

166 [SOURCE: Page_166]

29   Introduction [SOURCE: Page_167]

30   Understanding the factors that regulate the assembly of communities across space and time is a main focus of [SOURCE: Page_167]

31   community ecology (Weiher & Keddy, 1999) and macroecology (Brown, 1995). The theory of habitat filters [SOURCE: Page_167]

32   states that local lake fish assemblages are selected from regional species pools through various sieves acting at [SOURCE: Page_167]

33   different spatial and temporal scales (Tonn, 1990; Poff, 1997). Therefore, local assemblages are immediate [SOURCE: Page_167]

34   products of local and contemporary processes, but if the effects of regional and historical filters are strong, then [SOURCE: Page_167]

35   the impact of these larger-scale factors can be significant (Tonn et al., 1990). The theory of landscape filters was [SOURCE: Page_167]

36   proposed to understand the large-scale diversity in community compositions (Bellwood & Hughes, 2001; [SOURCE: Page_167]

37   Bellwood et al., 2002; Bremner et al., 2003, 2006; Hoeinghaus et al., 2007). [SOURCE: Page_167]

38   Some ecological theories such as the habitat templet theory (Southwood, 1977, 1988) and its derivation to [SOURCE: Page_167]

39   freshwater systems (Townsend & Hildrew, 1994) states that habitat, and especially its temporal dynamic, is the [SOURCE: Page_167]

40   main driving force selecting species and thus structuring local communities. Numerous studies conducted at [SOURCE: Page_167]

41   different spatial scales support these hypotheses (Townsend et al., 1997; Grossman et al., 1998; Logez et al., [SOURCE: Page_167]

42   2013). Other theories suggest that biotic interactions drive community structures and could for instance explain [SOURCE: Page_167]

43   the longitudinal patterns of diversity observed toward the equator (Schemske et al., 2009). Nevertheless, it is [SOURCE: Page_167]

44   commonly acknowledged that these two factors act conjointly to shape local communities (Gido et al., 2009). [SOURCE: Page_167]

45   The spatial structure of fish communities is a function of abiotic and biotic factors such as hydrology (Poff and [SOURCE: Page_167]

46   Allan 1995), interspecific competition or predation (Horwitz, 1978; Matthews, 1998; Jackson et al., 2001; Begon [SOURCE: Page_167]

47   et al., 2006). Abiotic and biotic factors both control the spatiotemporal variability of local community structure [SOURCE: Page_167]

48   (Brown, 1984; Cornwell & Ackerly, 2009). Among the factors acting at the local scale, the amount and quality [SOURCE: Page_167]

49   of habitats is of prime importance in explaining biodiversity. Indeed, habitat diversity, complexity and continuity [SOURCE: Page_167]

50   can impact different processes including growth, reproduction and mortality as well as dispersal and biotic [SOURCE: Page_167]

51   interactions (Tonn & Magnuson, 1982; Benson & Magnuson, 1992; Lienesch & Matthews, 2000; Marsh- [SOURCE: Page_167]

52   Matthews & Matthews, 2000). [SOURCE: Page_167]

53   In lentic systems, it is well known that depth, vegetation cover and substrate size are important in the spatial [SOURCE: Page_167]

54   segregation of fish in the littoral zone (Werner et al., 1977; Rossier, 1995; Fischer & Eckmann, 1997b; Gasith et [SOURCE: Page_167]

55   al., 2000; Laffaille et al., 2001; Lewin et al., 2004; Brosse et al., 2007; Kratochvıl et al., 2012). From a [SOURCE: Page_167]

56   hydrological point of view, reservoirs are at the boundary between streams and lakes, with their own functioning, [SOURCE: Page_167]

57   which is highly dependent on human uses resulting in irregular and extreme variations in physical factors [SOURCE: Page_167]

167 [SOURCE: Page_167]

58   (Wetzel, 1990). In these systems, water-level fluctuations (WLFs) are often a major determinant of the [SOURCE: Page_168]

59   ecosystem’s functioning (Wilcox & Meeker, 1991, 1992; Poff et al., 1997; Poirel et al., 2001) and the main [SOURCE: Page_168]

60   stressors for communities inhabiting the littoral area expose to temporal falling dry (Sutela & Vehanen, 2008). [SOURCE: Page_168]

61   Because they are interfacing aquatic and terrestrial systems, littoral zones have a greater heterogeneity of [SOURCE: Page_168]

62   physical structure (Schmieder, 2004; Zohary & Ostrovsky, 2011), and thus habitat, and greater trophic resources [SOURCE: Page_168]

63   than pelagic areas (Pierce et al., 1993). The littoral zone is crucial for the development of numerous species that [SOURCE: Page_168]

64   use these habitats to accomplish their biological cycle (Werner et al., 1977; Keast, 1985; Savino & Stein, 1989). [SOURCE: Page_168]

65   Most of the studies dedicated to the influence of WLFs in reservoirs have focused on the modifications of [SOURCE: Page_168]

66   physical conditions and on macrophyte communities (Gafny & Gasith, 2000; Riis & Hawes, 2002; Leira & [SOURCE: Page_168]

67   Cantonati, 2008). In Lake Kinneret and Lake Tahoe, the proportion of small particles increased, while the [SOURCE: Page_168]

68   proportion of gravel, stones and boulders decreased, as did littoral complexity (Gasith & Gafny, 1990; [SOURCE: Page_168]

69   Beauchamp et al., 1994; Zohary & Ostrovsky, 2011). With increasing WLFs, the littoral zones of deep lakes are [SOURCE: Page_168]

70   affected first, their habitat diversity declines, and consequently species richness and abundance (Zohary & [SOURCE: Page_168]

71   Ostrovsky, 2011). The WLF effects on fish have not been assessed directly and have been essentially inferred [SOURCE: Page_168]

72   from the alterations of their habitats (Leira & Cantonati, 2008). The loss of reproduction habitats or the decrease [SOURCE: Page_168]

73   in shelter availability following a water level drop, for example, has been well documented (Gafny et al., 1992; [SOURCE: Page_168]

74   Fischer & Ohl, 2005). Studying long time series, some authors have also shown that WLFs may impact [SOURCE: Page_168]

75   population dynamics: reproductive success is regularly correlated with the rates and magnitude of rising water [SOURCE: Page_168]

76   levels (Ostrovsky & Walline, 2000; Kahl et al., 2008; Webb, 2008). In addition, several studies have attempted [SOURCE: Page_168]

77   to evaluate the consequences of WLFs on fish communities by comparing highly contrasted systems with [SOURCE: Page_168]

78   different management modes, but comparisons are not really significant because variables such as nutrient level [SOURCE: Page_168]

79   and lake size also affect fish communities (James & Graynoth, 2002; Rowe et al., 2003; Sutela & Vehanen, [SOURCE: Page_168]

80   2008). Even if some studies have focused on WLF effects on habitat and communities, few studies have matched [SOURCE: Page_168]

81   the temporal dynamic of habitat conditions and the temporal dynamic of littoral zone community structure. [SOURCE: Page_168]

82   The main objective of this study was to analyze the concomitant changes in habitat characteristics and fish [SOURCE: Page_168]

83   community associated with the available habitats during the WLF period in the Bariousses reservoir. [SOURCE: Page_168]

84   Environmental characteristics and fish community relationships were studied here at a single site but in different [SOURCE: Page_168]

85   water-level contexts. This reservoir designed for electricity production constitutes a consistent case study to [SOURCE: Page_168]

86   assess the relative importance of the WLFs. Indeed, water-level stability and variability alternate over a short [SOURCE: Page_168]

87   temporal scale, which provides favorable conditions to study the effects of WLF by limiting the potential effects [SOURCE: Page_168]

168 [SOURCE: Page_168]

88    of confounding factors that could blur the biological signals. In summer, the water level is relatively stable to [SOURCE: Page_169]

89    support touristic activities (fluctuations less than 0.5 m), but beginning in September the WLFs increase to [SOURCE: Page_169]

90    satisfy intensified electricity demand. The heterogeneity of the reservoir’s morphology (Fig. 1) and of the littoral [SOURCE: Page_169]

91    habitats support the value of studying the Bariousses reservoir, because the WLF effects are expected to differ [SOURCE: Page_169]

92    over the spatial extent of the reservoir. In addition, the fish community of this reservoir is relatively diverse, with [SOURCE: Page_169]

93    14 species known, which is common in French reservoirs (Irz et al., 2006). [SOURCE: Page_169]

94    To achieve these goals, the environmental characteristics and of the fish communities of the littoral zone were [SOURCE: Page_169]

95    described. We first assessed the influence of WLF on shore habitats and then on fish communities. The second [SOURCE: Page_169]

96    step consisted in studying the relative influence of environmental characteristics on fish communities at different [SOURCE: Page_169]

97    water levels using multivariate analyses. The working hypothesis postulated that littoral habitat will become [SOURCE: Page_169]

98    increasingly homogeneous as the water level of the reservoir decreases and consequently following the habitat [SOURCE: Page_169]

99    templet theory, fish communities will respond to this homogenization by also becoming more and more [SOURCE: Page_169]

100   homogenous. [SOURCE: Page_169]

101 [SOURCE: Page_169]

169 [SOURCE: Page_169]

102   Methods [SOURCE: Page_170]

103   Study site [SOURCE: Page_170]

104   Bariousses reservoir is an 80.9-ha impoundment of the Vézère River in west central France (45.33°N, 1.49°E) [SOURCE: Page_170]

105   (Fig. 1) operated by Electricité de France. Its perimeter is 9.9 km, its mean and maximum depths are 7.1 m and [SOURCE: Page_170]

106   18.9 m, respectively. This reservoir is monomictic with a period of summer stratification. In 2011, the water [SOURCE: Page_170]

107   level fluctuated within an altitudinal range of 507.2 to 513.5 mNgF with a mean of 511 mNgF (Fig. 2). This [SOURCE: Page_170]

108   amplitude is regularly observed at the inter-annual scale (EDF, personal source). [SOURCE: Page_170]

109   Fish sampling procedure [SOURCE: Page_170]

110   Thirty sampling sites throughout the reservoir were selected to represent a variety of habitats in the littoral zone. [SOURCE: Page_170]

111   They were characterized by 45 m of shoreline (46.7 ± 14 m) with homogenous habitat (Gelwick & Matthews, [SOURCE: Page_170]

112   1990) with the depth between 0 and 1.5 m to maximize the efficiency of electrofishing (Gelwick & Matthews, [SOURCE: Page_170]

113   1990; Brosse et al., 2007). Fish were sampled monthly between July and October 2011, i.e., 19–21 July, 23–25 [SOURCE: Page_170]

114   August, 20–22 September, and 18–20 October, during daylight hours by electrofishing (EFKO FEG 8000, [SOURCE: Page_170]

115   Germany) using a continuous voltage between 300 and 600 V. A total of 120 samples (30 sites*4 months) were [SOURCE: Page_170]

116   collected. Fish species caught were determined and individuals of each species were classified into "ecospecies" [SOURCE: Page_170]

117   representing either young-of-the-year (0+) or older fish (hereafter referred to as adults) depending on their size [SOURCE: Page_170]

118   (Brosse et al., 2007). To avoid observation of temporal trends that often occur in fish abundance data, fish [SOURCE: Page_170]

119   samples were characterized by the presence/absence of all the ecospecies collected (Brosse et al., 2007). [SOURCE: Page_170]

120   Environmental characterization [SOURCE: Page_170]

121   During the fish sampling period, the water level decreased by 2.4 m (EDF, personal source). It was 511.64 ± [SOURCE: Page_170]

122   0.07 m NgF in July, 511.38 ± 0.07 mNgF in August, 510.15 ± 0.01 mNgF in September, and 508.98 ± 0.06 m [SOURCE: Page_170]

123   NgF in October (Fig. 2). Moreover, each fish sampling site was described just after the fish sampling using seven [SOURCE: Page_170]

124   environmental variables (four categorical and three numeric) known to influence fish distribution. The substrate [SOURCE: Page_170]

125   was characterized according to the Cailleux & Tricart (1959) classification and then categorized into "thin" (< 2 [SOURCE: Page_170]

126   mm) (T-Sub) and "coarser" (> 2 mm) (C-Sub) groups. Vegetation was categorized by two variables: presence of [SOURCE: Page_170]

127   submerged vegetation (tree branches, flooded grasses, and helophytes) (P-Sub-Veg) and presence of riparian [SOURCE: Page_170]

128   shade (P-Ri-Sh) (Gelwick & Matthews, 1990; Broadmeadow et al., 2011). The sampling period matched the [SOURCE: Page_170]

129   development of both aquatic and terrestrial vegetation. Slope was classified into three classes, i.e., low (L-Slo), [SOURCE: Page_170]

170 [SOURCE: Page_170]

130   medium (M-Slo), and high (H-Slo), whereas depth (D) was described by an average value over the whole [SOURCE: Page_171]

131   sampling site. Surface temperature (Temp-S) and oxygen concentration (Oxy) describing physical chemistry [SOURCE: Page_171]

132   were also collected during the sampling (WTW Oxi 197i). Finally, we recorded the water temperature with a [SOURCE: Page_171]

133   data logger located in the central zone 0.5 m deep throughout the study period (NKE - S2T600 with an accuracy [SOURCE: Page_171]

134   ± 0.05°C between 0 and +25°C). Subsequently, the sampling period will be designated by the month and this [SOURCE: Page_171]

135   term includes all the above mentioned environmental characteristics measured. [SOURCE: Page_171]

136   Data processing [SOURCE: Page_171]

137   The data set was composed of 120 samples with both environmental and biological data collected. We evaluated [SOURCE: Page_171]

138   the relationship between environmental variables with a Spearman rank correlation coefficient between numeric [SOURCE: Page_171]

139   environmental variables and a chi-square test between categorical environmental variables. Multivariate [SOURCE: Page_171]

140   approaches provided an objective approach to identifying patterns in species assemblages and their relationships [SOURCE: Page_171]

141   with environmental conditions (Jackson et al., 2001). We used a principal component analysis (PCA) to [SOURCE: Page_171]

142   simultaneously analyze categorical and numeric environmental variables (Hill & Smith, 1976). A PCA based on [SOURCE: Page_171]

143   a variance–covariance matrix was used to analyze in the third part the faunistic matrix. The differences in [SOURCE: Page_171]

144   environmental characteristics and fish communities between months were assessed with intergroup analyses. [SOURCE: Page_171]

145   Then, for each month, we studied the relation between environmental characteristics and fish communities with a [SOURCE: Page_171]

146   co-inertia analysis (Doledec & Chessel, 1994; Franquet et al., 1995; Verneaux et al., 1995; Castella & Speight, [SOURCE: Page_171]

147   1996). Co-inertia is a multivariate analysis that studies the link between two matrices by giving the same weight [SOURCE: Page_171]

148   to each table, as opposed to CCA, which explains one matrix by the second one. To quantify the link between the [SOURCE: Page_171]

149   two matrices, we also used the RV coefficient, which is an extension of the correlation coefficient to two [SOURCE: Page_171]

150   matrices (Robert & Escoufier, 1976). It ranges from 0 to 1. Finally, we performed a Monte-Carlo Test on the [SOURCE: Page_171]

151   sum of eigen values of the co-inertia analysis (999 random permutations) to check the significance of the [SOURCE: Page_171]

152   resulting correlation (Heo & Gabriel, 1997). All calculations and graphics were made using the R software (R [SOURCE: Page_171]

153   Development Core Team, 2008) and the ade^4 package (Thioulouse et al., 1997). [SOURCE: Page_171]

154 [SOURCE: Page_171]

171 [SOURCE: Page_171]

155   Results [SOURCE: Page_172]

156 [SOURCE: Page_172]

157   A. Characterization of the littoral habitats [SOURCE: Page_172]

158 [SOURCE: Page_172]

159   The water level decreased over the sampling period (Fig. 2), first slightly between July and August (−0.26 m), [SOURCE: Page_172]

160   and then increased in speed to reach its lowest level in October (−2.66 m compared to July). The habitat [SOURCE: Page_172]

161   conditions changed with the WLFs, in particular the attribute related to vegetation and substrate: submerged [SOURCE: Page_172]

162   vegetation, shade due to riparian vegetation, and to a lesser extent coarse substrate became rare in September and [SOURCE: Page_172]

163   October. Conversely, the sites with a low slope became the most abundant in October (Table 1). [SOURCE: Page_172]

164 [SOURCE: Page_172]

165   Average surface temperature and oxygen concentration in the littoral zone varied between the four periods, from [SOURCE: Page_172]

166   16.4 to 24.5°C and from 8.5 to 9.7mg.L−1 respectively (Table 2). These two parameters varied significantly [SOURCE: Page_172]

167   between the periods studied (ANOVA, p < 0.001). The water temperature in the littoral zone warmed between [SOURCE: Page_172]

168   July and August and then started cooling in September, but a slight warming was observed in October. Overall, [SOURCE: Page_172]

169   the reservoir warmed between July and August and then started to cool, as revealed by the variations in the mean [SOURCE: Page_172]

170   surface temperature of the central zone (Fig. 3). Temperatures in the central zone during sampling were very [SOURCE: Page_172]

171   close to the littoral temperatures for the warmest months, but diverged for the two coldest months (−2°C for [SOURCE: Page_172]

172   September and +5°C for October, compared to the littoral zone) (Fig. 3). [SOURCE: Page_172]

173 [SOURCE: Page_172]

174   Oxygen concentration and water temperature were highly redundant. Similarly, the slope was highly related to [SOURCE: Page_172]

175   the type of substrate and riparian shade. Oxygen concentration and the slope were not used for the multivariate [SOURCE: Page_172]

176   analyses. The first two PCA axes explained 75% of the total variability of the environmental matrix [SOURCE: Page_172]

177   (respectively, 45 and 30%) (Fig. 4A). The first axis represented a hydromorphological gradient associated with a [SOURCE: Page_172]

178   gradient of shade. It opposed the deepest sites with riparian shade and coarse substrate to sites with fine substrate [SOURCE: Page_172]

179   and no riparian shade. The second axis contrasted sites with or without submerged vegetation according to a [SOURCE: Page_172]

180   temperature gradient (Fig. 4B). The PCA revealed a shift of environmental conditions over time, supported by [SOURCE: Page_172]

181   the between-month inertia of 34% (permutation test, p = 0.001). These differences were driven by the shift of [SOURCE: Page_172]

182   habitat and water temperature. August and September displayed the two most contrasted environmental [SOURCE: Page_172]

183   conditions (Fig. 4C). The first factorial plane of the PCA also revealed a relative homogenization of the habitat [SOURCE: Page_172]

184   conditions in September and October as shown by the higher proximity of the samples compared to summer [SOURCE: Page_172]

172 [SOURCE: Page_172]

185   samples. However, some sites still had coarse substrate and riparian shade, observations that became rare in [SOURCE: Page_173]

186   September and October. [SOURCE: Page_173]

187 [SOURCE: Page_173]

188   B. Fish population changes in the littoral sites studied [SOURCE: Page_173]

189 [SOURCE: Page_173]

190   Seven species representing ten ecospecies, four juveniles and six adults, with an occurrence greater than 5% [SOURCE: Page_173]

191   were sampled (Table 3). Pumpkinseed (0+ and adult), adult perch, and 0+ rudd were the most frequently [SOURCE: Page_173]

192   observed ecospecies on the sites. The first factorial plane of the PCA on the faunistic matrix explained 40% of [SOURCE: Page_173]

193   the total inertia (Fig. 5A). The ordinations of the ecospecies displayed two gradients: the juveniles on the first [SOURCE: Page_173]

194   axis and the adults on the second one. The relative orthogonality of these two axes indicated that occurrences of [SOURCE: Page_173]

195   juveniles and adults are not related (Fig. 5B). In contrast to the environmental conditions, there were no temporal [SOURCE: Page_173]

196   differences in species occurrences as demonstrated by the low between-month inertia (4.3%, permutation test, p [SOURCE: Page_173]

197   = 0.01) (Fig. 5C). [SOURCE: Page_173]

198 [SOURCE: Page_173]

199   C. Relations between environmental conditions and fish assemblages [SOURCE: Page_173]

200 [SOURCE: Page_173]

201   The relations between environmental conditions and species occurrences differed between months, as revealed [SOURCE: Page_173]

202   by the RV coefficients. The lowest relations between environment and species were observed for July and [SOURCE: Page_173]

203   October, with RV equal to 0.27 and 0.18 (Monte-Carlo tests, p < 0.01 and > 0.05 respectively), whereas the [SOURCE: Page_173]

204   highest relation was observed for August followed by September, with RV values equal to 0.42 and 0.35, [SOURCE: Page_173]

205   respectively (Monte-Carlo tests, p = 0.001). These results are reinforced by the co-inertia results. For each [SOURCE: Page_173]

206   month, the first factorial plane of the co-inertia explained more than 87% of the total inertia (87.2–93.5%), but [SOURCE: Page_173]

207   the co-structure of the environmental and faunistic matrices were always mainly explained by the first axis. The [SOURCE: Page_173]

208   simultaneous projections of the environment and species matrices on the first factorial plane of the co-inertia [SOURCE: Page_173]

209   (arrows on Fig. 6) made it possible to identify sites with a high concordance between species composition and [SOURCE: Page_173]

210   environmental conditions, symbolized by short arrows, and sites with high discrepancies between species and [SOURCE: Page_173]

211   environment, symbolized by long arrows. With few exceptions, for August and September the base and head of [SOURCE: Page_173]

212   arrows were close, revealing a consistent relation between environment and species composition. For July and [SOURCE: Page_173]

213   especially October, numerous arrows with relatively distant starting points shared the same end points. This [SOURCE: Page_173]

214   means that sites with very different habitat conditions had very similar species compositions. In addition, in [SOURCE: Page_173]

173 [SOURCE: Page_173]

215   September and October, some sites had significantly different habitats and fish compositions. Therefore, the [SOURCE: Page_174]

216   strength of the environmental conditions and species occurrence relationships varied with WLFs. [SOURCE: Page_174]

217 [SOURCE: Page_174]

218   Discussion [SOURCE: Page_174]

219   In accordance with previous results, we expected a change in the littoral habitat characteristics of the Bariousses [SOURCE: Page_174]

220   reservoir induced by WLFs. This study shows that the habitat conditions tended to be much more homogeneous [SOURCE: Page_174]

221   as the water level decreased significantly and that some habitats disappeared. Concurrently with these physical [SOURCE: Page_174]

222   modifications, the link between environment and local assemblage composition changed, from low for the [SOURCE: Page_174]

223   highest and lowest water levels to high for the two intermediate water levels observed between 19 July and 20 [SOURCE: Page_174]

224   October 2011. [SOURCE: Page_174]

225   Open areas with fine substrate provide a low structural complexity, offering no refuge and fewer food resources [SOURCE: Page_174]

226   than coarse substrate, which provide intermediate complexity, while habitats with vegetation are known to have [SOURCE: Page_174]

227   a greater diversity of life forms due to their higher complexity (Gasith & Gafny, 1998; Meerhoff et al., 2007; [SOURCE: Page_174]

228   Brauns et al., 2008; Zohary & Ostrovsky, 2011). All these habitats participate in the high diversity of littoral [SOURCE: Page_174]

229   habitats with diverse levels of structural complexity (Gasith & Gafny, 1990; Dembski et al., 2008). In this [SOURCE: Page_174]

230   reservoir, the complex habitats were mainly available for fish during the highest water levels, close to the mean [SOURCE: Page_174]

231   annual water level, i.e., during July and August. During the two following sampling periods, the majority of the [SOURCE: Page_174]

232   habitats were composed of thin substrate without submerged vegetation and riparian shade. Therefore, habitat [SOURCE: Page_174]

233   structural complexity dropped sharply when the water level decreased below 1 m from this mean annual water [SOURCE: Page_174]

234   level, then remained stable when the water level continued to drop. This decrease in complexity was also [SOURCE: Page_174]

235   observed in Lake Kinneret where the percentage of shoreline covered by small particles increased from 6% to [SOURCE: Page_174]

236   49% with a 3-m decrease in water level. Conjointly with the increase in small particles, the proportion of coarse [SOURCE: Page_174]

237   substrate declined, and thus also littoral habitat complexity (Gasith & Gafny, 1990; Beauchamp et al., 1994; [SOURCE: Page_174]

238   Zohary & Ostrovsky, 2011). Decreasing water levels tend to reduce habitat diversity in the reservoir and the [SOURCE: Page_174]

239   degree of change is highly dependent on local conditions observed on-site. However, exposure of new thin [SOURCE: Page_174]

240   substrate during extreme low-water periods facilitated the expansion of emergent plants (Gasith & Gafny, 1990; [SOURCE: Page_174]

241   Hudon, 1997; Leira & Cantonati, 2008) and young-of-the-year fish used them for refuges and foraging habitat [SOURCE: Page_174]

242   when the water level rose (Rozas & Odum, 1988; Savino & Stein, 1989; Eklöv, 1997; Dembski et al., 2008; [SOURCE: Page_174]

243   Midwood & Chow-Fraser, 2012). [SOURCE: Page_174]

174 [SOURCE: Page_174]

244   Moreover, despite a sharp decline in habitat complexity on most littoral sites, few isolated sites of coarse [SOURCE: Page_175]

245   substrate, with an intermediate structural complexity, were present whatever the water-level decrease, i.e., 1 and [SOURCE: Page_175]

246   2 m below the mean annual water level in 2011. These sites were the only source of refuge available for fish in [SOURCE: Page_175]

247   these water-level conditions (Fischer & Ohl, 2005), which may explain why they were composed of very [SOURCE: Page_175]

248   specific communities. Coarse habitats play a crucial role in reservoirs with WLFs. For instance, Gasith et al. [SOURCE: Page_175]

249   (2000) showed that species richness, abundance and biomass increased in areas with boulders with increasing [SOURCE: Page_175]

250   water levels because more fish and fish of a larger size range are attracted to this habitat. [SOURCE: Page_175]

251   Surface temperatures considered in this study reflect local conditions in the littoral zone during sampling. [SOURCE: Page_175]

252   Sampling occasions were quite different in the thermal context, which can be explained by season and weather [SOURCE: Page_175]

253   effects. The disappearance of the riparian shade in the littoral zone when the water level decreased in September [SOURCE: Page_175]

254   and October could explain the differences between littoral and central temperature. Several studies have shown [SOURCE: Page_175]

255   that riparian shade has a substantial impact on the thermal regime by reducing diel variability and temperature [SOURCE: Page_175]

256   extremes (France, 1997; Malcolm et al., 2004; Tanentzap et al., 2008; Broadmeadow et al., 2011). [SOURCE: Page_175]

257   Our biological results add to the understanding of the consequences of WLFs on fish assemblages in the littoral [SOURCE: Page_175]

258   zone of reservoirs. Fish data were collected by electrofishing, which allowed us to sample fish in complex or [SOURCE: Page_175]

259   intermediate structures at a depth where it was difficult or impossible to sample by seining or gill netting [SOURCE: Page_175]

260   (Gelwick & Matthews, 1990). This method did not allow using abundance data, a limitation to our analysis. [SOURCE: Page_175]

261   Alternative methods could be used to acquire abundance data (Eggers et al., 1978; Fischer & Eckmann, 1997a) [SOURCE: Page_175]

262   and to confirm or refute the results observed for presence/absence data. Nevertheless, the spatial segregation [SOURCE: Page_175]

263   between young and adult fish observed with the PCA is a very well-known pattern in lentic systems (Duncan & [SOURCE: Page_175]

264   Kubecka, 1995; Fischer & Eckmann, 1997b) and especially reservoirs (Matena, 1995; Brosse et al., 2007). This [SOURCE: Page_175]

265   confirms the advantages of our data and the consistency of the results. [SOURCE: Page_175]

266   The environmental variability (temporal changes in habitat availability) induced by WLFs here seems to be the [SOURCE: Page_175]

267   main driver of assemblage structure, as observed by Grossman et al. (1998) and predicted by the habitat templet [SOURCE: Page_175]

268   theory (Townsend & Hildrew, 1994). RV coefficients, as correlation coefficients, could not be directly compared [SOURCE: Page_175]

269   two by two (the samples were not exactly the same), but they give a valuable estimation of the strength of the [SOURCE: Page_175]

270   relations between environment and assemblage. The relation between habitat diversity and assemblage structure [SOURCE: Page_175]

271   was not as pronounced here. Indeed, the habitat effect on assemblage structure was strongest when the water [SOURCE: Page_175]

272   level decreased by 0.26 and 1.49 m (greatest RVs) and lowest for the lowest water level. Habitat diversity was [SOURCE: Page_175]

175 [SOURCE: Page_175]

273   the highest for the highest water level, but the magnitude of the relation between environmental conditions and [SOURCE: Page_176]

274   assemblage structure was only the third. This suggests that temporal variability of environmental conditions had [SOURCE: Page_176]

275   a greater influence on fish assemblage structures of the littoral zone than habitat diversity (Grossman et al., [SOURCE: Page_176]

276   1998), as predicted by the habitat templet theory (Townsend & Hildrew, 1994). This is also corroborated by the [SOURCE: Page_176]

277   patterns observed on the PCA. While the sites were closer on the PCA on habitat conditions, the sites were [SOURCE: Page_176]

278   increasingly homogeneous as the water level decreased; ordinations on the PCA based on fish data did not really [SOURCE: Page_176]

279   differ between time periods. During intermediate water-level periods, habitat was clearly the major factor [SOURCE: Page_176]

280   structuring littoral assemblages, whereas, during the highest and lowest water levels, habitat was overcome by [SOURCE: Page_176]

281   other factors such as interspecific competition, a shift in microhabitat uses, or random distributions of certain [SOURCE: Page_176]

282   individuals (Bystrom et al., 1998; Grossman et al., 1998; Fischer & Kummer, 2000; Persson et al., 2004; Brosse [SOURCE: Page_176]

283   et al., 2007). Some studies showed that intra- and interspecific interactions are common among littoral fishes [SOURCE: Page_176]

284   (Mittelbach, 1986; Pierce et al., 1993). [SOURCE: Page_176]

285   This study provides highly valuable and innovative results on the spatiotemporal dynamics of fish assemblages [SOURCE: Page_176]

286   in different water-level contexts. Our results showed that WLFs influenced habitat availability and that [SOURCE: Page_176]

287   complexity tended to decrease with decreasing water levels. Moreover, we observed a temporal dynamic in the [SOURCE: Page_176]

288   environment–fish relationships in the Bariousses reservoir, but water level does not seem to be the only [SOURCE: Page_176]

289   structuring factor. [SOURCE: Page_176]

290   In addition to the variations observed at the assemblage levels, we are convinced that it would be highly valuable [SOURCE: Page_176]

291   to study the response of fish individuals to WLFs to see how their behavior could be modified during important [SOURCE: Page_176]

292   phases of their biological cycle or to see how they can shift their microhabitat uses by moving within the [SOURCE: Page_176]

293   reservoir in search of available habitats. Such studies could lead to suggestions for managing the reservoirs that [SOURCE: Page_176]

294   satisfy both the biological needs of the biocenosis inhabiting the Bariousses reservoirs and hydroelectricity [SOURCE: Page_176]

295   production. [SOURCE: Page_176]

296   Acknowledgements: [SOURCE: Page_176]

297   We warmly thank Tiphaine Peroux from Irstea Aix-en-Provence for her assistance in the field. Our thanks are [SOURCE: Page_176]

298   also extended to Electricité De France, which manages the Treignac hydroelectric dam with its Bariousses [SOURCE: Page_176]

299   reservoir. [SOURCE: Page_176]

ANNEXE III: Article 2 [SOURCE: Page_191]

Testing the VEMCO Positioning System: spatial distribution of the probability of location and the positioning error in a reservoir [SOURCE: Page_191]

(Animal Biotelemetry) [SOURCE: Page_191]

191 [SOURCE: Page_191]

Testing the VEMCO Positioning System: spatial distribution of the probability of location and the positioning error in a reservoir Roy et al. [SOURCE: Page_192]

Roy et al. Animal Biotelemetry 2014, 2:1 http://www.animalbiotelemetry.com/content/2/1/1 [SOURCE: Page_192]

192 [SOURCE: Page_192]

Roy et al. Animal Biotelemetry 2014, 2:1 http://www.animalbiotelemetry.com/content/2/1/1 [SOURCE: Page_193]

SHORT COMMUNICATION                                                                                                                          Open Access [SOURCE: Page_193]

Testing the VEMCO Positioning System: spatial distribution of the probability of location and the positioning error in a reservoir Romain Roy^1*, Jeremy Beguin^2, Christine Argillier^2, Laurence Tissot^1, Frank Smith^3, Stephanie Smedbol^3 and Eric De-Oliveira^1 [SOURCE: Page_193]

Abstract Background: Recent improvements in fixed acoustic monitoring receivers allow the tracking of individual aquatic animals over long periods of time with regular fine-scale positions. The VEMCO Positioning System (VPS) is now widely used, but various methodological issues remain to be clarified. The aim of this study was to analyze the spatial distribution of the probability of location and the positioning error over the entire surface of a hydropower reservoir, prior to analyzing fish behavior. Findings: Filtering the data set by the horizontal position error (HPE) significantly reduced the positioning error. Retaining only the positions with an HPE less than 15 retained 79% of VPS positions and decreased the positioning error by 33% (mean = 3.3 m, SD = 3.3 m). A higher probability of location was observed inside than outside the receiver array (44% and 36%, respectively). Moreover, the positioning error significantly differed inside (n = 243, mean = 2.4 m, SD = 2.1 m) and outside (n = 253, mean = 4.2 m, SD = 4.0 m) the receiver array (P < 0.001). Finally, the lowest positioning errors were detected in the area with the highest receiver density. Conclusions: The VPS measures fish positioning in a reservoir, under suitable conditions, with satisfactory accuracy. We showed that the probability of location and the positioning error differed spatially in accordance with previous results in other conditions. Consequently, these analyses are recommended as a prerequisite to further spatial analyses using VPS-derived data. Keywords: Acoustic telemetry, VEMCO Positioning System, Probability of location, Positioning error, Spatial distribution [SOURCE: Page_193]

Findings                                                                            behavioral data collected, the accuracy of positioning mea- Background                                                                          surements is seldom assessed (but see [6-8]). Its shape is irregular and its water level used by the VEMCO Radio-Acoustic Positioning (VRAP)                           fluctuates. An array of 40 underwater VR2W omnidirec- system [9,13]. The VPS has been extended to work with                         tional acoustic receivers (VEMCO, Halifax, NS, Canada) an array of three or more receivers that do not have clocks                   was deployed in January 2012 throughout the reservoir synchronized to real time [9,13]. This system is                     the whole reservoir, while seeking to provide full local de- now widely used in fish behavior studies in marine and                        tection coverage and high precision of estimated locations. freshwater environments [7,14-20]. However, its perform-                      The receiver deployment took the bathymetry, the shape ance has not yet been extensively documented (but see                         of the reservoir and the maximum intensity of water-level [7]). The aim of this study was to analyze the spatial distri-                fluctuations into account to avoid the potential for re- bution of the probability of location and the positioning                     ceivers being beached. ANNEXE IV: Le problème de décompression rapide chez les poissons [SOURCE: Page_199]

La vessie natatoire des poissons est un organe majeur servant d’appareil hydrostatique (Blanchard 1880). Elle leur permet de compenser le gradient de pression occasionné par la colonne d’eau. Lorsque les individus sont remontés rapidement des grandes profondeurs (pêche au filet ou à la ligne), un problème de décompression rapide peut être occasionné. Il engendre alors une surproduction de gaz dans la vessie natatoire et les vaisseaux sanguins (Nguyen, Gravel et al. 2009). Cette vessie natatoire augmente alors brutalement de volume et le poisson est incapable d’avoir un équilibre de nage correct. Les conséquences physiologiques et physiques peuvent être considérables avec par exemple, un élargissement de la vessie natatoire, des comportements de nage anormaux, des hémorragies, ou une hausse de la pression oculaire (Suski, Killen et al. 2004; Morrissey, Suski et al. 2005; Rummer and Bennett 2005). Une étude de télémétrie a récemment pu révéler que ce phénomène peut entraîner la mort des individus (Gravel and Cooke 2008). [SOURCE: Page_199]

Une méthode dénommée « fizzing » est couramment mise en œuvre pour limiter les conséquences de ce phénomène. Elle consiste à percer la vessie natatoire avec une aiguille hypodermique. L’aiguille, orientée avec un angle de 45° vers la tête, est insérée dans la vessie natatoire à une profondeur d’environ 10-15 mm. Le point d’insertion de l’aiguille doit être localisé entre la limite de la nageoire pectorale et la ligne latérale. L’immersion du poisson sous l’eau permet de bien vérifier que des gaz s’échappent de la vessie. Le positionnement optimal du site de « fizzing » variant selon les espèces, il est primordial avant toute intervention de s’informer et de pratiquer sur l’espèce ciblée (Nguyen, Gravel et al. 2009). Lorsque le « fizzing » ne peut être pratiqué, un relâcher immédiat du poisson, plutôt qu’une stabulation, peut diminuer les risques de dommages physiologiques mais n’éliminera pas les effets de la décompression rapide (Nguyen, Gravel et al. 2009). [SOURCE: Page_199]

Dans la littérature, les effets du « fizzing » sur le comportement des poissons ont été évalués à partir d’une étude de télémétrie (Nguyen, Gravel et al. 2009). Le « fizzing » n’a pas engendré de mortalité, n’a pas eu d’effet significatif sur le taux de mouvement et le temps de dispersion post-marquage (Suski, Killen et al. 2004; Nguyen, Gravel et al. 2009). [SOURCE: Page_199]

Dans le cadre de cette étude, 20 sandres et perches ont présenté les symptômes de la décompression rapide. Ils ont été capturés à la ligne ou à l’aide de filet, dans des profondeurs comprises entre 5 et 15 m, lorsque la température de fond était comprise entre 5.5°C et 9°C. La méthode du « fizzing » a alors été appliquée en positionnant l’aiguille entre la limite de la nageoire pectorale et la ligne latérale. La méthode a été inefficace pour 3 individus, probablement en raison d’une intervention trop tardive. Par contre, grâce à cette méthode, 17 individus (11 sandres et 6 perches) ont pu retrouver un comportement normal, être marqués puis relâchés après un séjour de quelques heures en stabulation. Le suivi télémétrique de ces individus a montré que l’intervention a été un succès pour 100% de ces individus. De plus, l’analyse des déplacements verticaux a montré que ces individus sont de nouveau capables d’effectuer des mouvements au sein de la colonne d’eau quelques jours après l’intervention. [SOURCE: Page_199]

ANNEXE IX: Exemples de migration verticale réalisée par deux individus en période estivale, sandre T35 (en haut) et perche T42 (en bas). [SOURCE: Page_205]

La hauteur du poisson au dessus du fond (correspondant à la hauteur totale de la colonne d’eau retranché de la pression hydrostatique) a été moyennée pour chaque heure. Les couleurs correspondent aux phases de la journée (jour en noir, crépuscule en rouge, nuit en vert, aube en bleu) [SOURCE: Page_205]
